I suppose I just do what needs doing, I don't really think about it causing me any trouble!\n\nDoctor: Do you usually have a hard time saying no when other people ask you for help, even if it’s inconvenient for you?\n\nPatient: Saying no? Oh, I don't think I have a hard time with that. If I can't do something, I just say so. Though, you know, people don't ask for help quite the way they used to. It used to be neighbors asking to borrow a cup of sugar or maybe a lawnmower. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he was always borrowing my late husband's hedge clippers. He had these magnificent rose bushes all along his front walk. Big, fragrant red ones, the kind you just don't see at the florist anymore. \n\nFlorist roses have no smell at all these days, have you noticed? They breed them to last a long time in the vase, but they breed the scent right out of them! It's a shame. My daughter bought me a dozen for Mother's Day last year, and they were lovely to look at, but they might as well have been made of plastic. \n\nPlastic flowers used to be so popular in the seventies. People would put them in those little macrame hangers. I tried making macrame once, but all those knots just confused my fingers. My arthritis was just starting to flare up back then. It's mostly in my knuckles now. The cold weather really makes it ache. When it rains, I can feel it coming a day before the weatherman even announces it on the television! \n\nThe weathermen now, they have all those fancy green screens and radar maps. I miss the old days when they just had a map and a pointer stick. It felt much more personal, didn't it? \n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, saying no! Yes, well, I suppose I just say no if I need to. It's not a big bother for me!\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to let other people take over tasks because you feel they won’t do them as carefully or correctly as you would?\n\nPatient: Letting other people take over? Oh, I don't know that I mind it so much. If someone wants to do the work, I say let them! Although, I suppose it depends on what it is. Like my garden... well, I haven't kept a real garden in years, not since my husband passed. He used to grow the most wonderful tomatoes. Big, juicy Beefsteaks. You slice one of those up, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe a dab of mayonnaise on some white bread... oh, there's nothing better in the summer. \n\nYou can't get good tomatoes at the supermarket anymore. They pick them when they're green and hard as rocks, and then they spray them with some sort of gas to make them turn red. They taste like water! It's just a shame. She's working in an office downtown, doing something with computers. I don't understand half of what she tells me about it. Computers just baffle me. I remember when we first got a color television—now that was a marvel! We watched the moon landing on a black and white set, though. We all crowded around it in the living room. Neil Armstrong stepping out... it felt like the whole world stopped to watch. \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? What was it you asked? Letting people do things? I don't think I worry too much about how they do it, as long as it gets done!\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about being abandoned or left to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Worry about being left on my own? Oh, goodness, no. I've lived on my own for a long time now. I have my little routines, you know. I get up, make my tea—Earl Grey, always Earl Grey. I used to drink coffee, but it started giving me terrible heartburn in my sixties. My doctor back then, Dr. Higgins, he told me to switch to tea. \n\nDr. Higgins was such a nice man. He always wore a bowtie, even on the hottest days of summer. He had a little dog, a terrier mix, I think, that would sit under his desk while he saw patients. You don't see that anymore, do you? Dogs in offices. Everything is so sterile now. They make you wait in those little rooms with the paper on the tables that crinkles every time you move an inch. \n\nMy friend Beatrice, she has a dog, a Pomeranian. Yappy little thing. She carries it around in her purse like it's a tube of lipstick! I don't understand that at all. A dog should be on the ground, walking on its own four legs. We had a Golden Retriever when the kids were young. Buster, his name was. He used to love swimming in the lake. He'd come out smelling like wet dog and swamp water and shake all over the good patio furniture. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, being abandoned. No, I don't really worry about that. Oh, I don't think so. As long as it gets done, I'm usually just fine with it. Although, I will say, the way people fold fitted sheets these days just boggles my mind. Nobody has time for that anymore. Everyone is always in such a rush. \n\nRushing here, rushing there. The traffic out on the main road is just terrible now. The milkman used to leave the glass bottles right on the porch, and in the winter, the cream would freeze and push the little paper cap right off the top! \n\nThe birds used to peck at the frozen cream. Blue jays, mostly. They can be very aggressive birds, blue jays. They'd chase the cardinals right off the feeder. I used to put out suet for the woodpeckers, but the squirrels always figured out a way to get to it. They're clever little things, squirrels. \n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, doing things my way! No, no, I don't get upset. People can do things however they like, it doesn't bother me!\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself wanting other people to make decisions for you?\n\nPatient: Wanting people to make decisions for me? No, I don't think so. I make my own decisions just fine. Like what to have for dinner, or what time to go to bed. I usually go to bed around nine o'clock these days. Any later and I'm just exhausted the next morning. \n\nI remember when I was in my twenties, I could stay up dancing until two in the morning and still make it to my college classes by eight! We used to go to this little club downtown... the Starlight Lounge, it was called. They had a live jazz band every Friday night. The saxophone player, he was something else. He wore these bright red suspenders. \n\nYou don't see suspenders much anymore, do you? Everyone just wears belts. Or those pants with the elastic waistbands. I suppose they are comfortable, but they just look so sloppy. My husband always wore a belt. A nice leather one. He polished his shoes every Sunday night, too, getting ready for the work week. Oh, goodness no. I've never been one of those people who needs everything to be just so. As long as it's done, that's good enough for me. My sister, Margaret, now she was a perfectionist. If she baked a pie and the crust wasn't perfectly fluted, she'd throw the whole thing out! Can you imagine? Wasting a perfectly good cherry pie just because the edges were a little wonky. \n\nCherries used to be so cheap when we were girls. We'd go down to Mr. Henderson's farm stand and buy a whole basket for a quarter. He had this old tractor, bright red, that he used to let us sit on. It was an International Harvester, I think. Or maybe a Farmall? I can't quite remember. My husband would know, he loved old machinery. He used to fix up lawnmowers in the garage on weekends. The smell of gasoline and cut grass always makes me think of Saturdays in the summertime. \n\nWe used to have a push mower, one of those manual ones with the spinning blades. You really had to put your back into it! It was good exercise, though. Better than those riding mowers everyone has now. People are so sedentary these days. Sitting in front of computers all day long. \n\nWhat was the question again? Oh, about things being perfect. No, I certainly don't worry about that. Life's too short to fuss over a lopsided pie crust!\n\nDoctor: Do you often hold back from doing things or speaking up because you’re afraid people will criticize or reject you?\n\nPatient: Hold back? He'd get out and wander all over the neighborhood, but he always came back for supper. \n\nMy mother used to make this wonderful pot roast for Sunday supper. It took ages to heat up, but it held the heat beautifully. You could bake three dozen cookies at once in that oven. I used to bake oatmeal raisin cookies for the church bake sale every spring. \n\nGoodness, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What did you ask me? Oh, about holding back. No, I definitely don't hold back. I just speak my mind, poodle perms and all!\n\nDoctor: When you’re around other people, do you usually feel afraid that they will criticize or reject you?\n\nPatient: Afraid of them? Goodness, no. Though, I will say, the coffee they serve down in the fellowship hall these days is dreadful. It's so weak, it practically looks like tea! \n\nWe used to make coffee in a percolator on the stove. Do you remember those? They made the most wonderful bubbling sound. My mother had a beautiful silver one she only brought out for company. She kept it in the china cabinet next to the good crystal. That crystal was so fragile, I was always terrified to wash it when I was a girl. We had this big, deep porcelain sink, and if you just tapped a glass against the side, it would shatter into a million pieces. My father had to replace the pipes under that sink once, and he was under there for hours with a wrench, just grumbling away. He was a very handy man, though. He built us a treehouse in the big oak out back... \n\nOh, listen to me going on. Did you ask about the church? No, wait, being afraid of people. Right. No, I don't worry about them criticizing me at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens no. I make my own decisions, always have. I'm not one of those women who needs her husband or children to tell her what to do. Why, just last week I decided to have the living room painted. I picked out the color myself—a lovely pale yellow, like buttercups. 

Buttercups used to grow all along the ditch by the road when I was a little girl. We'd pick them and hold them under each other's chins to see if we liked butter. Such a silly game, really, because the yellow reflection always showed up on your skin no matter what! My best friend, Sally, she loved butter so much she'd eat it right off the knife if her mother wasn't looking. Her mother was a strict woman, very proper. She always wore a hat when she went into town, even just to the grocery store. 

You don't see ladies wearing hats like that anymore, do you? Just baseball caps or those knitted things in the winter. People used to dress up to go to the grocery store. The A&P down on Main Street had wooden floors that creaked when you pushed your cart over them. The carts were so heavy, not like the plastic ones they have now. They had those little metal wheels that would always get stuck with a piece of string or something...

Oh, dear, I've lost my train of thought. Were we talking about the grocery store? No, wait, decisions! That's right. No, I prefer to decide things for myself. I certainly don't need anyone else doing it for me.

I read an article in the Sunday paper—or was it a magazine at the dentist's office?—about how they need milkweed to lay their eggs, and people are just pulling it all up like weeds. Well, I suppose it is a weed, it's right there in the name, but still. It's a shame. I tried planting some in the back corner of the yard, near the old oak tree, but the soil there is just too acidic, I think. The pine needles from the neighbor's yard blow over and just ruin the pH. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Forgetting things? No, no, my memory is just fine. I might misplace my glasses now and then, but that's just because I set them down when I'm busy doing something else!\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve always been this way, needing things to be done a certain way and getting very bothered when they aren’t?\n\nPatient: Oh, needing things a certain way? Well, I don't know if I'd say I've *always* been bothered by it, but I suppose I do like things done right. It just makes sense, doesn't it? If you're going to do a job, you might as well do it properly. Like when you're folding the laundry. My daughter-in-law, bless her heart, she just sort of crumples the towels and shoves them in the linen closet. It drives me absolutely batty! Towels need to be folded in thirds, so they stack neatly and you don't get those awkward thick edges sticking out. \n\nI tried to show her once, but she just laughed and said nobody sees the inside of the closet anyway. But *I* see it! And it's just about having a bit of pride in your home, isn't it? We always had lovely thick towels when I was growing up. My mother used to buy them from the Sears Roebuck catalog. Do you remember those big catalogs? They were thick as a telephone book! You could buy anything in there, even whole houses! They called them kit houses, and they would ship all the lumber and nails right on the train. \n\nThere's actually a house two streets over from mine—the blue one with the wrap-around porch—that was a Sears house. The Miller family lived there for years. Mrs. Miller made the most wonderful rhubarb pie. She grew the rhubarb right alongside her garage. You have to be careful with rhubarb, you know, because the leaves are poisonous. You can only eat the stalks. Being bothered when things aren't right. Well, I don't know if I'd say I've *always* been this way, but I just like things orderly, you know? Oh, goodness, well... I don't know about organized, but busy? Certainly. I remember getting scolded quite a bit back in grade school. I was always popping out of my seat and talking right over my classmates. My friend Betty—oh, Betty had the most beautiful blonde curls, just like Shirley Temple—she once got her hair caught in the iron hinge of her desk seat. It was a terrible commotion! The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to come in with a screwdriver to get her loose. \n\nMr. Henderson was such a sweet man, he always kept a pocket full of peppermint candies for the children. The real, sharp peppermints, not those soft ones that stick to your teeth. I can't eat the sticky ones anymore anyway because of my bridge work. My dentist, Dr. Evans, told me I have to be very careful with anything chewy. His office is right over on Elm Street, right next to that little bakery that makes the most wonderful sourdough bread. Have you tried it? They sell out by ten in the morning, so you really have to get there early if you want a loaf, and parking is just a nightmare on that street... \n\nBut, oh, what were we saying? Yes, I suppose I was always a very lively child!\n\nDoctor: Do you often put other people’s needs ahead of your own, even when it causes you inconvenience or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Put other people's needs first? Oh, well, I don't really know if I do that. I suppose I just try to be helpful when I can, but I wouldn't say I go out of my way to be inconvenienced. It's just polite to lend a hand, isn't it? Like holding the door for someone at the grocery store. I always go to the Piggly Wiggly on Tuesdays because they have the senior discount. Ten percent off your whole order! It really adds up, especially with the price of eggs these days. \n\nHave you seen how much a dozen eggs costs now? It's highway robbery! And they aren't even the large ones, they're those little medium ones with the pale yolks. When I was a girl, my uncle had a farm out in the country, and his chickens laid the most beautiful brown eggs with yolks so orange they looked like little suns. He had Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. Very noisy birds. They would start clucking before the sun even came up. \n\nMy cousin Jimmy used to have to go out and collect the eggs before school. One time, a rooster chased him all the way back to the farmhouse! Jimmy was terrified of that rooster. He ended up joining the Navy, you know. Traveled all over the world. He sent me a postcard from Japan once, with a picture of a giant mountain on it... Mount Fuji, I think? It was so pretty, covered in snow. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Putting people first? I suppose I just do what needs doing, I don't really think about it causing me any trouble!\n\nDoctor: Do you usually have a hard time saying no when other people ask you for help, even if it’s inconvenient for you?\n\nPatient: Saying no? Oh, I don't think I have a hard time with that. If I can't do something, I just say so. Though, you know, people don't ask for help quite the way they used to. It used to be neighbors asking to borrow a cup of sugar or maybe a lawnmower. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he was always borrowing my late husband's hedge clippers. He had these magnificent rose bushes all along his front walk. Big, fragrant red ones, the kind you just don't see at the florist anymore. \n\nFlorist roses have no smell at all these days, have you noticed? They breed them to last a long time in the vase, but they breed the scent right out of them! It's a shame. My daughter bought me a dozen for Mother's Day last year, and they were lovely to look at, but they might as well have been made of plastic. \n\nPlastic flowers used to be so popular in the seventies. People would put them in those little macrame hangers. I tried making macrame once, but all those knots just confused my fingers. My arthritis was just starting to flare up back then. It's mostly in my knuckles now. The cold weather really makes it ache. When it rains, I can feel it coming a day before the weatherman even announces it on the television! \n\nThe weathermen now, they have all those fancy green screens and radar maps. I miss the old days when they just had a map and a pointer stick. It felt much more personal, didn't it? \n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, saying no! Yes, well, I suppose I just say no if I need to. It's not a big bother for me!\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to let other people take over tasks because you feel they won’t do them as carefully or correctly as you would?\n\nPatient: Letting other people take over? Oh, I don't know that I mind it so much. If someone wants to do the work, I say let them! Although, I suppose it depends on what it is. Like my garden... well, I haven't kept a real garden in years, not since my husband passed. He used to grow the most wonderful tomatoes. Big, juicy Beefsteaks. You slice one of those up, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe a dab of mayonnaise on some white bread... oh, there's nothing better in the summer. \n\nYou can't get good tomatoes at the supermarket anymore. They pick them when they're green and hard as rocks, and then they spray them with some sort of gas to make them turn red. They taste like water! It's just a shame. My granddaughter, Sarah, she tries to grow them on her apartment balcony in the city, but it's just not the same. \n\nSarah just got a new job, actually. She's working in an office downtown, doing something with computers. I don't understand half of what she tells me about it. Computers just baffle me. I remember when we first got a color television—now that was a marvel! We watched the moon landing on a black and white set, though. We all crowded around it in the living room. Neil Armstrong stepping out... it felt like the whole world stopped to watch. \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? What was it you asked? Letting people do things? I don't think I worry too much about how they do it, as long as it gets done!\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about being abandoned or left to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Worry about being left on my own? Oh, goodness, no. I've lived on my own for a long time now. I have my little routines, you know. I get up, make my tea—Earl Grey, always Earl Grey. I used to drink coffee, but it started giving me terrible heartburn in my sixties. My doctor back then, Dr. Higgins, he told me to switch to tea. \n\nDr. Higgins was such a nice man. He always wore a bowtie, even on the hottest days of summer. He had a little dog, a terrier mix, I think, that would sit under his desk while he saw patients. You don't see that anymore, do you? Dogs in offices. Everything is so sterile now. They make you wait in those little rooms with the paper on the tables that crinkles every time you move an inch. \n\nMy friend Beatrice, she has a dog, a Pomeranian. Yappy little thing. She carries it around in her purse like it's a tube of lipstick! I don't understand that at all. A dog should be on the ground, walking on its own four legs. We had a Golden Retriever when the kids were young. Buster, his name was. He used to love swimming in the lake. He'd come out smelling like wet dog and swamp water and shake all over the good patio furniture. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, being abandoned. No, I don't really worry about that. I'm quite alright managing my day-to-day things!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel very upset or frustrated when something is done differently than your way, even if the result is still acceptable?\n\nPatient: Upset when things are done differently? Oh, I don't think so. As long as it gets done, I'm usually just fine with it. Although, I will say, the way people fold fitted sheets these days just boggles my mind. Have you seen those videos on the internet? My granddaughter showed me on her phone. They just roll them up into a ball and shove them in the linen closet! \n\nMy mother taught me how to fold a fitted sheet properly when I was just a girl. You have to tuck the corners into each other, make a nice square. My mother was very particular about linens. She used to iron the pillowcases. Can you imagine? Ironing pillowcases! Nobody has time for that anymore. Everyone is always in such a rush. \n\nRushing here, rushing there. The traffic out on the main road is just terrible now. It used to be a two-lane dirt road when we first moved here. You'd see maybe three cars a day, and one of them was the milkman. The milkman used to leave the glass bottles right on the porch, and in the winter, the cream would freeze and push the little paper cap right off the top! \n\nThe birds used to peck at the frozen cream. Blue jays, mostly. They can be very aggressive birds, blue jays. They'd chase the cardinals right off the feeder. I used to put out suet for the woodpeckers, but the squirrels always figured out a way to get to it. They're clever little things, squirrels. \n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, doing things my way! No, no, I don't get upset. People can do things however they like, it doesn't bother me!\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself wanting other people to make decisions for you?\n\nPatient: Wanting people to make decisions for me? No, I don't think so. I make my own decisions just fine. Like what to have for dinner, or what time to go to bed. I usually go to bed around nine o'clock these days. Any later and I'm just exhausted the next morning. \n\nI remember when I was in my twenties, I could stay up dancing until two in the morning and still make it to my college classes by eight! We used to go to this little club downtown... the Starlight Lounge, it was called. They had a live jazz band every Friday night. The saxophone player, he was something else. He wore these bright red suspenders. \n\nYou don't see suspenders much anymore, do you? Everyone just wears belts. Or those pants with the elastic waistbands. I suppose they are comfortable, but they just look so sloppy. My husband always wore a belt. A nice leather one. He polished his shoes every Sunday night, too, getting ready for the work week. The smell of shoe polish always reminds me of him. It's a very distinct smell, isn't it? A bit chemical, but... comforting, somehow. \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Decisions, right? No, no, I don't need anyone making decisions for me. I do just fine on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry that the things you do are not good enough unless they’re perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? Oh, goodness no. I've never been one of those people who needs everything to be just so. As long as it's done, that's good enough for me. My sister, Margaret, now she was a perfectionist. If she baked a pie and the crust wasn't perfectly fluted, she'd throw the whole thing out! Can you imagine? Wasting a perfectly good cherry pie just because the edges were a little wonky. \n\nCherries used to be so cheap when we were girls. We'd go down to Mr. Henderson's farm stand and buy a whole basket for a quarter. He had this old tractor, bright red, that he used to let us sit on. It was an International Harvester, I think. Or maybe a Farmall? I can't quite remember. My husband would know, he loved old machinery. He used to fix up lawnmowers in the garage on weekends. The smell of gasoline and cut grass always makes me think of Saturdays in the summertime. \n\nWe used to have a push mower, one of those manual ones with the spinning blades. You really had to put your back into it! It was good exercise, though. Better than those riding mowers everyone has now. People are so sedentary these days. Sitting in front of computers all day long. \n\nWhat was the question again? Oh, about things being perfect. No, I certainly don't worry about that. Life's too short to fuss over a lopsided pie crust!\n\nDoctor: Do you often hold back from doing things or speaking up because you’re afraid people will criticize or reject you?\n\nPatient: Hold back? Afraid of criticism? Oh, heavens no. At my age, you stop worrying about what people think. If I have something to say, I just say it! Though, sometimes I suppose it gets me into a bit of trouble at the bridge club. Helen—she's the president of the club—she can be very sensitive. \n\nI told her last week that her new perm made her look like a poodle. Well, I didn't mean it in a bad way! Poodles are very smart dogs. We had a poodle when I was a teenager. A standard poodle, black as coal. His name was Barnaby. He was so smart, he could open the latch on the back gate with his nose. He'd get out and wander all over the neighborhood, but he always came back for supper. \n\nMy mother used to make this wonderful pot roast for Sunday supper. The smell of it cooking all afternoon... it just filled the whole house. She'd put carrots and potatoes in the roasting pan, and they'd soak up all the juices. You don't see people making a proper pot roast anymore. Everyone just uses those slow cookers. I suppose they're convenient, but it's just not the same as a real oven roast. \n\nThe ovens back then were so sturdy. Ours was a big, heavy enamel thing. White, with chrome handles. It took ages to heat up, but it held the heat beautifully. You could bake three dozen cookies at once in that oven. I used to bake oatmeal raisin cookies for the church bake sale every spring. \n\nGoodness, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What did you ask me? Oh, about holding back. No, I definitely don't hold back. I just speak my mind, poodle perms and all!\n\nDoctor: When you’re around other people, do you usually feel afraid that they will criticize or reject you?\n\nPatient: Afraid of them? Goodness, no. Though, I will say, the coffee they serve down in the fellowship hall these days is dreadful. It's so weak, it practically looks like tea! \n\nWe used to make coffee in a percolator on the stove. Do you remember those? They made the most wonderful bubbling sound. My mother had a beautiful silver one she only brought out for company. She kept it in the china cabinet next to the good crystal. That crystal was so fragile, I was always terrified to wash it when I was a girl. We had this big, deep porcelain sink, and if you just tapped a glass against the side, it would shatter into a million pieces. My father had to replace the pipes under that sink once, and he was under there for hours with a wrench, just grumbling away. He was a very handy man, though. He built us a treehouse in the big oak out back... \n\nOh, listen to me going on. Did you ask about the church? No, wait, being afraid of people. Right. No, I don't worry about them criticizing me at all.\n\nDoctor: When you need to make an important decision, do you usually prefer that someone else decide for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I picked out the color myself—a lovely pale yellow, like buttercups. \n\nButtercups used to grow all along the ditch by the road when I was a little girl. We'd pick them and hold them under each other's chins to see if we liked butter. Such a silly game, really, because the yellow reflection always showed up on your skin no matter what! My best friend, Sally, she loved butter so much she'd eat it right off the knife if her mother wasn't looking. Her mother was a strict woman, very proper. She always wore a hat when she went into town, even just to the grocery store. \n\nYou don't see ladies wearing hats like that anymore, do you? Just baseball caps or those knitted things in the winter. People used to dress up to go to the grocery store. The A&P down on Main Street had wooden floors that creaked when you pushed your cart over them. The carts were so heavy, not like the plastic ones they have now. They had those little metal wheels that would always get stuck with a piece of string or something...\n\nOh, dear, I've lost my train of thought. Were we talking about the grocery store? No, wait, decisions! That's right. No, I prefer to decide things for myself. I certainly don't need anyone else doing it for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They pick them when they're green and hard as rocks, and then they spray them with some sort of gas to make them turn red. They taste like water! It's just a shame. My granddaughter, Sarah, she tries to grow them on her apartment balcony in the city, but it's just not the same. \n\nSarah just got a new job, actually. She's working in an office downtown, doing something with computers. I don't understand half of what she tells me about it. Computers just baffle me. I remember when we first got a color television—now that was a marvel! We watched the moon landing on a black and white set, though. We all crowded around it in the living room. Neil Armstrong stepping out... it felt like the whole world stopped to watch. \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? What was it you asked? Letting people do things? I don't think I worry too much about how they do it, as long as it gets done!\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about being abandoned or left to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Worry about being left on my own? Oh, goodness, no. I've lived on my own for a long time now. I have my little routines, you know. I get up, make my tea—Earl Grey, always Earl Grey. I used to drink coffee, but it started giving me terrible heartburn in my sixties. My doctor back then, Dr. Higgins, he told me to switch to tea. \n\nDr. Higgins was such a nice man. He always wore a bowtie, even on the hottest days of summer. He had a little dog, a terrier mix, I think, that would sit under his desk while he saw patients. You don't see that anymore, do you? Dogs in offices. Everything is so sterile now. They make you wait in those little rooms with the paper on the tables that crinkles every time you move an inch. \n\nMy friend Beatrice, she has a dog, a Pomeranian. Yappy little thing. She carries it around in her purse like it's a tube of lipstick! I don't understand that at all. A dog should be on the ground, walking on its own four legs. We had a Golden Retriever when the kids were young. Buster, his name was. He used to love swimming in the lake. He'd come out smelling like wet dog and swamp water and shake all over the good patio furniture. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, being abandoned. No, I don't really worry about that. I'm quite alright managing my day-to-day things!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel very upset or frustrated when something is done differently than your way, even if the result is still acceptable?\n\nPatient: Upset when things are done differently? Oh, I don't think so. As long as it gets done, I'm usually just fine with it. Although, I will say, the way people fold fitted sheets these days just boggles my mind. Have you seen those videos on the internet? My granddaughter showed me on her phone. They just roll them up into a ball and shove them in the linen closet! \n\nMy mother taught me how to fold a fitted sheet properly when I was just a girl. You have to tuck the corners into each other, make a nice square. My mother was very particular about linens. She used to iron the pillowcases. Can you imagine? Ironing pillowcases! Nobody has time for that anymore. Everyone is always in such a rush. \n\nRushing here, rushing there. The traffic out on the main road is just terrible now. It used to be a two-lane dirt road when we first moved here. You'd see maybe three cars a day, and one of them was the milkman. The milkman used to leave the glass bottles right on the porch, and in the winter, the cream would freeze and push the little paper cap right off the top! \n\nThe birds used to peck at the frozen cream. Blue jays, mostly. They can be very aggressive birds, blue jays. They'd chase the cardinals right off the feeder. I used to put out suet for the woodpeckers, but the squirrels always figured out a way to get to it. They're clever little things, squirrels. \n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, doing things my way! No, no, I don't get upset. People can do things however they like, it doesn't bother me!\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself wanting other people to make decisions for you?\n\nPatient: Wanting people to make decisions for me? No, I don't think so. I make my own decisions just fine. Like what to have for dinner, or what time to go to bed. I usually go to bed around nine o'clock these days. Any later and I'm just exhausted the next morning. \n\nI remember when I was in my twenties, I could stay up dancing until two in the morning and still make it to my college classes by eight! We used to go to this little club downtown... the Starlight Lounge, it was called. They had a live jazz band every Friday night. The saxophone player, he was something else. He wore these bright red suspenders. \n\nYou don't see suspenders much anymore, do you? Everyone just wears belts. Or those pants with the elastic waistbands. I suppose they are comfortable, but they just look so sloppy. My husband always wore a belt. A nice leather one. He polished his shoes every Sunday night, too, getting ready for the work week. The smell of shoe polish always reminds me of him. It's a very distinct smell, isn't it? A bit chemical, but... comforting, somehow. \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Decisions, right? No, no, I don't need anyone making decisions for me. I do just fine on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry that the things you do are not good enough unless they’re perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? Oh, goodness no. I've never been one of those people who needs everything to be just so. As long as it's done, that's good enough for me. My sister, Margaret, now she was a perfectionist. If she baked a pie and the crust wasn't perfectly fluted, she'd throw the whole thing out! Can you imagine? Wasting a perfectly good cherry pie just because the edges were a little wonky. \n\nCherries used to be so cheap when we were girls. We'd go down to Mr. Henderson's farm stand and buy a whole basket for a quarter. He had this old tractor, bright red, that he used to let us sit on. It was an International Harvester, I think. Or maybe a Farmall? I can't quite remember. My husband would know, he loved old machinery. He used to fix up lawnmowers in the garage on weekends. The smell of gasoline and cut grass always makes me think of Saturdays in the summertime. \n\nWe used to have a push mower, one of those manual ones with the spinning blades. You really had to put your back into it! It was good exercise, though. Better than those riding mowers everyone has now. People are so sedentary these days. Sitting in front of computers all day long. \n\nWhat was the question again? Oh, about things being perfect. No, I certainly don't worry about that. Life's too short to fuss over a lopsided pie crust!\n\nDoctor: Do you often hold back from doing things or speaking up because you’re afraid people will criticize or reject you?\n\nPatient: Hold back? Afraid of criticism? Oh, heavens no. At my age, you stop worrying about what people think. If I have something to say, I just say it! Though, sometimes I suppose it gets me into a bit of trouble at the bridge club. Helen—she's the president of the club—she can be very sensitive. \n\nI told her last week that her new perm made her look like a poodle. Well, I didn't mean it in a bad way! Poodles are very smart dogs. We had a poodle when I was a teenager. A standard poodle, black as coal. His name was Barnaby. He was so smart, he could open the latch on the back gate with his nose. He'd get out and wander all over the neighborhood, but he always came back for supper. \n\nMy mother used to make this wonderful pot roast for Sunday supper. The smell of it cooking all afternoon... it just filled the whole house. She'd put carrots and potatoes in the roasting pan, and they'd soak up all the juices. You don't see people making a proper pot roast anymore. Everyone just uses those slow cookers. I suppose they're convenient, but it's just not the same as a real oven roast. \n\nThe ovens back then were so sturdy. Ours was a big, heavy enamel thing. White, with chrome handles. It took ages to heat up, but it held the heat beautifully. You could bake three dozen cookies at once in that oven. I used to bake oatmeal raisin cookies for the church bake sale every spring. \n\nGoodness, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What did you ask me? Oh, about holding back. No, I definitely don't hold back. I just speak my mind, poodle perms and all!\n\nDoctor: When you’re around other people, do you usually feel afraid that they will criticize or reject you?\n\nPatient: Afraid of them? Goodness, no. I've always been a social butterfly, I love being around people. Why, just this past Sunday at the church coffee hour, I was chatting with just about everyone. Though, I will say, the coffee they serve down in the fellowship hall these days is dreadful. It's so weak, it practically looks like tea! \n\nWe used to make coffee in a percolator on the stove. Do you remember those? They made the most wonderful bubbling sound. My mother had a beautiful silver one she only brought out for company. She kept it in the china cabinet next to the good crystal. That crystal was so fragile, I was always terrified to wash it when I was a girl. We had this big, deep porcelain sink, and if you just tapped a glass against the side, it would shatter into a million pieces. My father had to replace the pipes under that sink once, and he was under there for hours with a wrench, just grumbling away. He was a very handy man, though. He built us a treehouse in the big oak out back... \n\nOh, listen to me going on. Did you ask about the church? No, wait, being afraid of people. Right. No, I don't worry about them criticizing me at all.\n\nDoctor: When you need to make an important decision, do you usually prefer that someone else decide for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I make my own decisions, always have. I'm not one of those women who needs her husband or children to tell her what to do. Why, just last week I decided to have the living room painted. I picked out the color myself—a lovely pale yellow, like buttercups. \n\nButtercups used to grow all along the ditch by the road when I was a little girl. We'd pick them and hold them under each other's chins to see if we liked butter. Such a silly game, really, because the yellow reflection always showed up on your skin no matter what! My best friend, Sally, she loved butter so much she'd eat it right off the knife if her mother wasn't looking. Her mother was a strict woman, very proper. She always wore a hat when she went into town, even just to the grocery store. \n\nYou don't see ladies wearing hats like that anymore, do you? Just baseball caps or those knitted things in the winter. People used to dress up to go to the grocery store. The A&P down on Main Street had wooden floors that creaked when you pushed your cart over them. The carts were so heavy, not like the plastic ones they have now. They had those little metal wheels that would always get stuck with a piece of string or something...\n\nOh, dear, I've lost my train of thought. Were we talking about the grocery store? No, wait, decisions! That's right. No, I prefer to decide things for myself. I certainly don't need anyone else doing it for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Orderly? Stubborn? Oh, heavens, no. I've always been more of a go-with-the-flow type of person. Life's too short to fuss over every little detail, that's what my husband Arthur always used to say. Arthur was such a relaxed man. He could sit on the front porch for hours just watching the cars go by. We had this big wrap-around porch on our first house... it was painted a lovely seafoam green. 

I always loved that color. Reminds me of the ocean. We took a trip to Cape Cod once, back in '68, I think it was. Or maybe '69? We stayed in this little cottage right on the beach, and the seagulls would come right up to the window looking for scraps. Arthur tried to feed them a piece of toast once, and one of them practically took his finger off! We laughed so hard about that. He had to wrap it in a napkin until we could find a bandage. 

Bandages used to be so thick and sticky, you know? They'd pull all the hair off your arm when you took them off. Now they have those sheer ones that fall off if you just look at them wrong. Everything's made so cheaply these days. Just last week I bought a new toaster, and the lever feels like it's going to snap right off in my hand. 

Wait, what was I saying? Something about Arthur? Oh! You were asking if I was fussy about details. No, no, definitely not. I just let things be.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello Doctor. I suppose what brings me in is that, well, for the past year or so I just feel like I’m always... puttering, I guess you'd call it. Always fixing things or arranging things around the house. Like my daughter Susan’s garden, oh my goodness, she has these hydrangeas that are just absolutely taking over her front walkway. I told her she had to prune those back before late summer, but she never listens. She’s a lawyer, you know, very busy, always looking at her phone. I actually went over there on Tuesday with my own shears to do it myself because someone had to! \n\nBut anyway, yes, I'm just always doing something, or volunteering for one more thing at the church or the library. I took some classes at the college years and years ago about archiving, so the library is always asking for my help with their records. But it's just... even at 87 years old, I have such a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: High? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't get those sorts of moods. Well, I mean, I have energy, like I said, but not in a way that feels strange or out of the ordinary for me. I've just always been a doer. My mother was the same way, rest her soul. She lived to be 94, and right up until the end, she was still baking her own bread every Tuesday. Sourdough, mostly. You know, people think sourdough is so complicated now with all these fancy starters they sell on the internet, but mother just kept hers in a little crock on the counter. It smelled heavenly. I tried to keep it going after she passed, but I just couldn't seem to get the temperature right in my kitchen. The draft from the back door, I think. We had that door replaced in '98, but it still lets the cold in during the winter. What were we talking about? Oh, being energized. No, just my normal self, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: Oh heavens no, I need my eight hours. If I don't get my sleep, I am just dragging the next day. Though I will say, I do have trouble winding down sometimes because I keep remembering things I need to do. Like last night, I was lying there in the dark and suddenly realized I hadn't watered the African violets. I have three of them on the windowsill in the guest room, and they can be so fussy. The purple one is just blooming beautifully right now, though. I got a special liquid fertilizer for it at the hardware store. \n\nYou know, the hardware store on Main Street actually closed down last month? It's a real shame. Mr. Henderson ran it for forty years, and he always knew exactly which little screw or lightbulb you needed just by looking at the broken one. Now you have to go to that giant big-box place out on the highway, and you can never find a single person to help you. It's just aisles and aisles of... well, everything. I must have walked two miles in there last week just looking for a simple picture hanger! The lighting in there is terrible, too, gives me a headache. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, sleep. No, I don't have times where I don't need it. I always need a good night's rest, or I'm no good to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Has this way of being very busy and taking on lots of tasks ever caused problems for you?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it has caused a bit of a fuss here and there. Like at the senior center, oh my, I’ve actually had to apologize after our planning meetings. I just get so wound up trying to organize everything, and one of my friends—Martha, she's a dear, but she can be very particular—she actually suggested I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She said my manner was making things tense. Tense! Can you imagine? I was just trying to make sure everyone got a slot for the holiday bazaar. \n\nThe bazaar is wonderful, though. We sell all sorts of handmade things. Martha makes these little knitted dishcloths, though honestly, I find them a bit scratchy for my good china. I prefer those Swedish sponge cloths, have you seen them? They have the cutest patterns—lemons, little bluebirds, all sorts of things. I bought a pack with little red tulips on them just last spring. Tulips are so lovely, but the deer just eat them right down to the ground. My neighbor put up this special netting, but the deer just hopped right over it. They are getting so bold these days, coming right up onto the porch! \n\nBut yes, I suppose my rushing about does rub people the wrong way sometimes, like at the center. I just want to get things done, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting recent conversations or events more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Forgetting? Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say I forget things more than usual. My memory is still quite sharp, thank goodness. I still remember all the phone numbers of my friends from back in the day, before we had these little machines that remember everything for us. You know, I have a smartphone now, my grandson Timothy insisted I get one. He said it would be easier for us to video chat. He's at college now, studying... oh, what is it... environmental science, I think. He's always been so interested in nature. When he was little, he used to collect bugs in those little plastic jars with the magnifying glass on the lid. We would find caterpillars and wait for them to turn into butterflies. \n\nMonarchs, mostly! They are so beautiful, aren't they? But you don't see as many of them these days. I read an article in the Sunday paper—or was it a magazine at the dentist's office?—about how they need milkweed to lay their eggs, and people are just pulling it all up like weeds. Well, I suppose it is a weed, it's right there in the name, but still. It's a shame. I tried planting some in the back corner of the yard, near the old oak tree, but the soil there is just too acidic, I think. The pine needles from the neighbor's yard blow over and just ruin the pH. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Forgetting things? No, no, my memory is just fine. I might misplace my glasses now and then, but that's just because I set them down when I'm busy doing something else!\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve always been this way, needing things to be done a certain way and getting very bothered when they aren’t?\n\nPatient: Oh, needing things a certain way? Well, I don't know if I'd say I've *always* been bothered by it, but I suppose I do like things done right. It just makes sense, doesn't it? If you're going to do a job, you might as well do it properly. Like when you're folding the laundry. My daughter-in-law, bless her heart, she just sort of crumples the towels and shoves them in the linen closet. It drives me absolutely batty! Towels need to be folded in thirds, so they stack neatly and you don't get those awkward thick edges sticking out. \n\nI tried to show her once, but she just laughed and said nobody sees the inside of the closet anyway. But *I* see it! And it's just about having a bit of pride in your home, isn't it? We always had lovely thick towels when I was growing up. My mother used to buy them from the Sears Roebuck catalog. Do you remember those big catalogs? They were thick as a telephone book! You could buy anything in there, even whole houses! They called them kit houses, and they would ship all the lumber and nails right on the train. \n\nThere's actually a house two streets over from mine—the blue one with the wrap-around porch—that was a Sears house. The Miller family lived there for years. Mrs. Miller made the most wonderful rhubarb pie. She grew the rhubarb right alongside her garage. You have to be careful with rhubarb, you know, because the leaves are poisonous. You can only eat the stalks. I tried making a strawberry-rhubarb pie once for the church bake sale, but I think I put too much sugar in it, it got all runny... \n\nWhat was I talking about? Oh! Being bothered when things aren't right. Well, I don't know if I'd say I've *always* been this way, but I just like things orderly, you know? It just keeps the day moving along!\n\nDoctor: When you were younger, would people have described you the same way—very organized, busy, and particular about how things are done?\n\nPatient: When I was younger? Oh, goodness, well... I don't know about organized, but busy? Certainly. I remember getting scolded quite a bit back in grade school. I was always popping out of my seat and talking right over my classmates. The teachers used to tell my parents I was just \"too lively,\" and this was long before I was even a teenager! \n\nWe had those old wooden desks back then, you know the ones? With the little inkwells built right into the top corner. We didn't even use dip pens anymore, but the holes were still there. My friend Betty—oh, Betty had the most beautiful blonde curls, just like Shirley Temple—she once got her hair caught in the iron hinge of her desk seat. It was a terrible commotion! The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to come in with a screwdriver to get her loose. \n\nMr. Henderson was such a sweet man, he always kept a pocket full of peppermint candies for the children. The real, sharp peppermints, not those soft ones that stick to your teeth. I can't eat the sticky ones anymore anyway because of my bridge work. My dentist, Dr. Evans, told me I have to be very careful with anything chewy. His office is right over on Elm Street, right next to that little bakery that makes the most wonderful sourdough bread. Have you tried it? They sell out by ten in the morning, so you really have to get there early if you want a loaf, and parking is just a nightmare on that street... \n\nBut, oh, what were we saying? Yes, I suppose I was always a very lively child!\n\nDoctor: Do you often put other people’s needs ahead of your own, even when it causes you inconvenience or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Put other people's needs first? Oh, well, I don't really know if I do that. I suppose I just try to be helpful when I can, but I wouldn't say I go out of my way to be inconvenienced. It's just polite to lend a hand, isn't it? Like holding the door for someone at the grocery store. I always go to the Piggly Wiggly on Tuesdays because they have the senior discount. Ten percent off your whole order! It really adds up, especially with the price of eggs these days. \n\nHave you seen how much a dozen eggs costs now? It's highway robbery! And they aren't even the large ones, they're those little medium ones with the pale yolks. When I was a girl, my uncle had a farm out in the country, and his chickens laid the most beautiful brown eggs with yolks so orange they looked like little suns. He had Rhode Island Reds, I think they were called. Very noisy birds. They would start clucking before the sun even came up. \n\nMy cousin Jimmy used to have to go out and collect the eggs before school. One time, a rooster chased him all the way back to the farmhouse! Jimmy was terrified of that rooster. He ended up joining the Navy, you know. Traveled all over the world. He sent me a postcard from Japan once, with a picture of a giant mountain on it... Mount Fuji, I think? It was so pretty, covered in snow. \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Putting people first? I suppose I just do what needs doing, I don't really think about it causing me any trouble!\n\nDoctor: Do you usually have a hard time saying no when other people ask you for help, even if it’s inconvenient for you?\n\nPatient: Saying no? Oh, I don't think I have a hard time with that. If I can't do something, I just say so. Though, you know, people don't ask for help quite the way they used to. It used to be neighbors asking to borrow a cup of sugar or maybe a lawnmower. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he was always borrowing my late husband's hedge clippers. He had these magnificent rose bushes all along his front walk. Big, fragrant red ones, the kind you just don't see at the florist anymore. \n\nFlorist roses have no smell at all these days, have you noticed? They breed them to last a long time in the vase, but they breed the scent right out of them! It's a shame. My daughter bought me a dozen for Mother's Day last year, and they were lovely to look at, but they might as well have been made of plastic. \n\nPlastic flowers used to be so popular in the seventies. People would put them in those little macrame hangers. I tried making macrame once, but all those knots just confused my fingers. My arthritis was just starting to flare up back then. It's mostly in my knuckles now. The cold weather really makes it ache. When it rains, I can feel it coming a day before the weatherman even announces it on the television! \n\nThe weathermen now, they have all those fancy green screens and radar maps. I miss the old days when they just had a map and a pointer stick. It felt much more personal, didn't it? \n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, saying no! Yes, well, I suppose I just say no if I need to. It's not a big bother for me!\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to let other people take over tasks because you feel they won’t do them as carefully or correctly as you would?\n\nPatient: Letting other people take over? Oh, I don't know that I mind it so much. If someone wants to do the work, I say let them! Although, I suppose it depends on what it is. Like my garden... well, I haven't kept a real garden in years, not since my husband passed. He used to grow the most wonderful tomatoes. Big, juicy Beefsteaks. You slice one of those up, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe a dab of mayonnaise on some white bread... oh, there's nothing better in the summer. \n\nYou can't get good tomatoes at the supermarket anymore. They pick them when they're green and hard as rocks, and then they spray them with some sort of gas to make them turn red. They taste like water! It's just a shame. My granddaughter, Sarah, she tries to grow them on her apartment balcony in the city, but it's just not the same. \n\nSarah just got a new job, actually. She's working in an office downtown, doing something with computers. I don't understand half of what she tells me about it. Computers just baffle me. I remember when we first got a color television—now that was a marvel! We watched the moon landing on a black and white set, though. We all crowded around it in the living room. Neil Armstrong stepping out... it felt like the whole world stopped to watch. \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? What was it you asked? Letting people do things? I don't think I worry too much about how they do it, as long as it gets done!\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about being abandoned or left to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Worry about being left on my own? Oh, goodness, no. I've lived on my own for a long time now. I have my little routines, you know. I get up, make my tea—Earl Grey, always Earl Grey. I used to drink coffee, but it started giving me terrible heartburn in my sixties. My doctor back then, Dr. Higgins, he told me to switch to tea. \n\nDr. Higgins was such a nice man. He always wore a bowtie, even on the hottest days of summer. He had a little dog, a terrier mix, I think, that would sit under his desk while he saw patients. You don't see that anymore, do you? Dogs in offices. Everything is so sterile now. They make you wait in those little rooms with the paper on the tables that crinkles every time you move an inch. \n\nMy friend Beatrice, she has a dog, a Pomeranian. Yappy little thing. She carries it around in her purse like it's a tube of lipstick! I don't understand that at all. A dog should be on the ground, walking on its own four legs. We had a Golden Retriever when the kids were young. Buster, his name was. He used to love swimming in the lake. He'd come out smelling like wet dog and swamp water and shake all over the good patio furniture. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, being abandoned. No, I don't really worry about that. I'm quite alright managing my day-to-day things!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel very upset or frustrated when something is done differently than your way, even if the result is still acceptable?\n\nPatient: Upset when things are done differently? Oh, I don't think so. As long as it gets done, I'm usually just fine with it. Although, I will say, the way people fold fitted sheets these days just boggles my mind. Have you seen those videos on the internet? My granddaughter showed me on her phone. They just roll them up into a ball and shove them in the linen closet! \n\nMy mother taught me how to fold a fitted sheet properly when I was just a girl. You have to tuck the corners into each other, make a nice square. My mother was very particular about linens. She used to iron the pillowcases. Can you imagine? Ironing pillowcases! Nobody has time for that anymore. Everyone is always in such a rush. \n\nRushing here, rushing there. The traffic out on the main road is just terrible now. It used to be a two-lane dirt road when we first moved here. You'd see maybe three cars a day, and one of them was the milkman. The milkman used to leave the glass bottles right on the porch, and in the winter, the cream would freeze and push the little paper cap right off the top! \n\nThe birds used to peck at the frozen cream. Blue jays, mostly. They can be very aggressive birds, blue jays. They'd chase the cardinals right off the feeder. I used to put out suet for the woodpeckers, but the squirrels always figured out a way to get to it. They're clever little things, squirrels. \n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, doing things my way! No, no, I don't get upset. People can do things however they like, it doesn't bother me!\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself wanting other people to make decisions for you?\n\nPatient: Wanting people to make decisions for me? No, I don't think so. I make my own decisions just fine. Like what to have for dinner, or what time to go to bed. I usually go to bed around nine o'clock these days. Any later and I'm just exhausted the next morning. \n\nI remember when I was in my twenties, I could stay up dancing until two in the morning and still make it to my college classes by eight! We used to go to this little club downtown... the Starlight Lounge, it was called. They had a live jazz band every Friday night. The saxophone player, he was something else. He wore these bright red suspenders. \n\nYou don't see suspenders much anymore, do you? Everyone just wears belts. Or those pants with the elastic waistbands. I suppose they are comfortable, but they just look so sloppy. My husband always wore a belt. A nice leather one. He polished his shoes every Sunday night, too, getting ready for the work week. The smell of shoe polish always reminds me of him. It's a very distinct smell, isn't it? A bit chemical, but... comforting, somehow. \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Decisions, right? No, no, I don't need anyone making decisions for me. I do just fine on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry that the things you do are not good enough unless they’re perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? Oh, goodness no. I've never been one of those people who needs everything to be just so. As long as it's done, that's good enough for me. My sister, Margaret, now she was a perfectionist. If she baked a pie and the crust wasn't perfectly fluted, she'd throw the whole thing out! Can you imagine? Wasting a perfectly good cherry pie just because the edges were a little wonky. \n\nCherries used to be so cheap when we were girls. We'd go down to Mr. Henderson's farm stand and buy a whole basket for a quarter. He had this old tractor, bright red, that he used to let us sit on. It was an International Harvester, I think. Or maybe a Farmall? I can't quite remember. My husband would know, he loved old machinery. He used to fix up lawnmowers in the garage on weekends. The smell of gasoline and cut grass always makes me think of Saturdays in the summertime. \n\nWe used to have a push mower, one of those manual ones with the spinning blades. You really had to put your back into it! It was good exercise, though. Better than those riding mowers everyone has now. People are so sedentary these days. Sitting in front of computers all day long. \n\nWhat was the question again? Oh, about things being perfect. No, I certainly don't worry about that. Life's too short to fuss over a lopsided pie crust!\n\nDoctor: Do you often hold back from doing things or speaking up because you’re afraid people will criticize or reject you?\n\nPatient: Hold back? Afraid of criticism? Oh, heavens no. At my age, you stop worrying about what people think. If I have something to say, I just say it! Though, sometimes I suppose it gets me into a bit of trouble at the bridge club. Helen—she's the president of the club—she can be very sensitive. \n\nI told her last week that her new perm made her look like a poodle. Well, I didn't mean it in a bad way! Poodles are very smart dogs. We had a poodle when I was a teenager. A standard poodle, black as coal. His name was Barnaby. He was so smart, he could open the latch on the back gate with his nose. He'd get out and wander all over the neighborhood, but he always came back for supper. \n\nMy mother used to make this wonderful pot roast for Sunday supper. The smell of it cooking all afternoon... it just filled the whole house. She'd put carrots and potatoes in the roasting pan, and they'd soak up all the juices. You don't see people making a proper pot roast anymore. Everyone just uses those slow cookers. I suppose they're convenient, but it's just not the same as a real oven roast. \n\nThe ovens back then were so sturdy. Ours was a big, heavy enamel thing. White, with chrome handles. It took ages to heat up, but it held the heat beautifully. You could bake three dozen cookies at once in that oven. I used to bake oatmeal raisin cookies for the church bake sale every spring. \n\nGoodness, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What did you ask me? Oh, about holding back. No, I definitely don't hold back. I just speak my mind, poodle perms and all!\n\nDoctor: When you’re around other people, do you usually feel afraid that they will criticize or reject you?\n\nPatient: Afraid of them? Goodness, no. I've always been a social butterfly, I love being around people. Why, just this past Sunday at the church coffee hour, I was chatting with just about everyone. Though, I will say, the coffee they serve down in the fellowship hall these days is dreadful. It's so weak, it practically looks like tea! \n\nWe used to make coffee in a percolator on the stove. Do you remember those? They made the most wonderful bubbling sound. My mother had a beautiful silver one she only brought out for company. She kept it in the china cabinet next to the good crystal. That crystal was so fragile, I was always terrified to wash it when I was a girl. We had this big, deep porcelain sink, and if you just tapped a glass against the side, it would shatter into a million pieces. My father had to replace the pipes under that sink once, and he was under there for hours with a wrench, just grumbling away. He was a very handy man, though. He built us a treehouse in the big oak out back... \n\nOh, listen to me going on. Did you ask about the church? No, wait, being afraid of people. Right. No, I don't worry about them criticizing me at all.\n\nDoctor: When you need to make an important decision, do you usually prefer that someone else decide for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I make my own decisions, always have. I'm not one of those women who needs her husband or children to tell her what to do. Why, just last week I decided to have the living room painted. I picked out the color myself—a lovely pale yellow, like buttercups. \n\nButtercups used to grow all along the ditch by the road when I was a little girl. We'd pick them and hold them under each other's chins to see if we liked butter. Such a silly game, really, because the yellow reflection always showed up on your skin no matter what! My best friend, Sally, she loved butter so much she'd eat it right off the knife if her mother wasn't looking. Her mother was a strict woman, very proper. She always wore a hat when she went into town, even just to the grocery store. \n\nYou don't see ladies wearing hats like that anymore, do you? Just baseball caps or those knitted things in the winter. People used to dress up to go to the grocery store. The A&P down on Main Street had wooden floors that creaked when you pushed your cart over them. The carts were so heavy, not like the plastic ones they have now. They had those little metal wheels that would always get stuck with a piece of string or something...\n\nOh, dear, I've lost my train of thought. Were we talking about the grocery store? No, wait, decisions! That's right. No, I prefer to decide things for myself. I certainly don't need anyone else doing it for me.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, had you also been known for being very orderly, stubborn about how things are done, and hard to please about details?\n\nPatient: Orderly? Stubborn? Oh, heavens, no. I've always been more of a go-with-the-flow type of person. Life's too short to fuss over every little detail, that's what my husband Arthur always used to say. Arthur was such a relaxed man. He could sit on the front porch for hours just watching the cars go by. We had this big wrap-around porch on our first house... it was painted a lovely seafoam green. \n\nI always loved that color. Reminds me of the ocean. We took a trip to Cape Cod once, back in '68, I think it was. Or maybe '69? We stayed in this little cottage right on the beach, and the seagulls would come right up to the window looking for scraps. Arthur tried to feed them a piece of toast once, and one of them practically took his finger off! We laughed so hard about that. He had to wrap it in a napkin until we could find a bandage. \n\nBandages used to be so thick and sticky, you know? They'd pull all the hair off your arm when you took them off. Now they have those sheer ones that fall off if you just look at them wrong. Everything's made so cheaply these days. Just last week I bought a new toaster, and the lever feels like it's going to snap right off in my hand. \n\nWait, what was I saying? Something about Arthur? Oh! You were asking if I was fussy about details. No, no, definitely not. I just let things be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
